## What actually didn't complete — and what did

**Nothing calibration-related failed.** Checked both:
- My own run (`output/calibration_run_20260902.json`): 68/68 candidates, `stopped_reason: completed`. Finished cleanly.
- Codex's ADANIENT runs: also technically completed each time — the problem there was degenerate *results* (score collapse, stuck candidates), not incompletion. Already reported.

**What actually didn't complete was the background Monitor I set up** (`bfjpvwz23`, watching for new output/anomalies). That's a process-lifecycle issue, not a calibration bug.

### Why it happened
A `Monitor`/background Bash task lives inside this CLI session's own process. When that process restarts or reconnects between turns (which is what happened here — new date banner, session boundary), any monitor from the *previous* process instance is orphaned: it isn't handed off, and the harness can't retroactively tell you how it ended. That's exactly what "no completion record found... may have been running when the previous process exited" means. It's a property of how in-session monitors work here, not something in the calibration code.

### Step-by-step remedial measures, scaled down
1. **Don't trust in-session monitors to outlive the session.** Treat them as best-effort for the current sitting only — useful for live narration, not as the system of record.
2. **Keep the actual work decoupled from the monitor.** This already holds: `CalibrationSupervisor` checkpoints every candidate to disk regardless of whether anything is watching it — so even if a monitor dies, no calibration progress is lost. Verified again just now: the checkpoint file is intact and complete.
3. **Re-establish state at the start of every session**, not just when asked — I did this today (checked git status, running processes, output directories) before you asked anything.
4. **For anything that must survive a process restart**, use `CronCreate` (a real OS-level scheduled job) instead of a live Monitor — I can set one up if you want ongoing, restart-proof checking rather than session-scoped watching.
5. Re-armed a fresh Monitor for this session; if you want it restart-proof going forward, tell me and I'll switch it to a cron-based check instead.

Want me to set up the cron-based version now?

## Deep-dive verdict

### Short answer
This repo is not yet ready for “10x mode / genius mode” parameter calibration as a live-trading, production-grade system.

It is only conditionally ready for a constrained offline calibration sandbox.

---

## Evidence in the repo

### 1) The safety layer is implemented and passing tests
The gate framework in gates_framework.py explicitly defines the 18-gate risk layer and its decision ordering. The project unittest suite in test_gates_framework.py is passing with fresh evidence:

- command run: `cd /home/shrinivas/ECS_Project && python3 -m unittest discover -q`
- result: “Ran 21 tests … OK”

So the execution safety layer is structurally valid and has unit coverage.

### 2) Calibration is intentionally separated from live risk infrastructure
calibration_config.py states the optimization search controls are separate from the live deployment config and that operational/risk gates should never be calibrated. That is a strong sign the project is designed to keep hard safety rails fixed while calibration only tunes search behavior.

This is healthy for a risk-first architecture, but it also means:
- calibration is not the same as “turning up the model”
- the repo is intentionally conservative, not a free-form “genius mode” system

### 3) The project is still described as a read-only observatory
README.md says this is a read-only local dashboard that does not:
- connect to real broker APIs
- place or cancel orders
- write bot state
- operate in live trading flow

That is important because a “10x mode” or “genius mode” readiness decision should be based on end-to-end trading execution, not just static gate logic.

---

## What this means

### Ready for:
- offline calibration experiments
- parameter-space exploration in sandbox
- risk-gate validation and regression testing
- controlled optimization within strict boundaries

### Not ready for:
- live-trading acceleration to “10x / genius mode”
- unrestricted parameter tuning on production-like flows
- broad autonomous optimization without walk-forward or OOS validation

---

## Core gap: no evidence of full calibration readiness
I do not see explicit repo evidence for:
- walk-forward validation across multiple market regimes
- out-of-sample performance gates before calibration
- mode-specific safeguards for “10x” or “genius”
- a documented deployment threshold for accepting parameter changes
- integration test covering real signal → entry decision → order lifecycle

So the real answer is:

> The revision-2 safety framework is ready enough to support controlled calibration work, but the complete project is not yet ready for aggressive “10x / genius mode” parameter calibration in live-trading terms.

---

## Recommended next step

Use a staged gate:

1. Sandbox calibration only
   - parameter tuning against historical data
   - require walk-forward and out-of-sample checks
   - hold all operational risk gates fixed

2. Pre-certification gate
   - no regression in safety tests
   - no deterioration in drawdown / exposure behavior
   - stable outputs across multiple seeds

3. Only then consider “10x” mode
   - after a full integrated validation pass
   - with explicit kill-switch, drawdown, and execution controls proven in end-to-end runs

If you want, I can do the next pass and produce a formal “Revision 2.0 readiness checklist” against the repo, with pass/fail gates for calibration approval.

## Correction to the earlier thread, and the actual 68-parameter map

Important finding first: everything the earlier "GitHub Copilot" transcript told you about the 68-parameter model ("not implemented," "only 33-35 params exist") is **wrong for the current state of the repo**. I re-checked the live files directly rather than trusting that history, and the full 68-parameter Revision 2 engine surface is actually built, wired to the black boxes, and passing tests:

- [canonical_parameter_registry.py](canonical_parameter_registry.py) — the frozen registry (`CanonicalParameterRegistry`)
- [calibration_config.py:92](calibration_config.py#L92) — `Revision2ParameterManifest` (`base_33()`, `revision2_35()`, `all_68()`, `hardcoded_20()`)
- [parameter_blackbox_bindings.py](parameter_blackbox_bindings.py) — maps every parameter to its owning black box
- [tests/test_canonical_parameter_registry.py](tests/test_canonical_parameter_registry.py) — 3/3 passing

Verified by running the registry itself:
```
total= 68
calibratable= 45
hardcoded= 20        # (see nuance below — this is a different 20 than the "45+23" split)
identity_sha256= ed1a74fa0f3edf8a89e471e6db40f992148862b240b77fdcdab7984578e35c70
```

### One nuance your "45 calibrate / ~20 hard-code" split glosses over
There are actually **two different "20"s** in the code, and they're not the same set:

1. Inside the 68: **45 calibratable** + **23 fixed-but-still-part-of-the-68** (`FIXED_TARGET_NAMES`) = 68. These 23 are engine-surface values (order type, trading hours, drawdown thresholds, symbol lists, etc.) that are deliberately excluded from the optimizer but still belong to the 68.
2. **A separate 20-item hard safety-invariant layer** (`kill_switch_enabled`, `max_daily_loss_rupees`, `max_concurrent_positions`, drawdown/lambda derate triggers, etc.) that lives outside the 68 entirely, enforced by `StartupCapabilityLock` / `SafetyGates` and never touched by calibration at all.

So the real structure is **68 (45 calibrate + 23 fixed) + 20 pure safety invariants = 88 total governed values**, not "68 = 45 + 20."

### Black-box distribution (from the actual bindings file)
| Black box | Param count | Role |
|---|---|---|
| PA | 21 | Predictive model layer |
| SafetyGates | 18 | Hard safety layer (spans both the 68 and the +20) |
| MPC | 17 | Exit/MPC control layer |
| P01D | 10 | Order execution layer |
| PositionManager | 9 | Sizing & exposure |
| UnifiedExecution | 6 | Orchestration/scheduling |
| DataIngestion | 2 | Universe/symbol filtering |
| ID | 3 | Judging/identification layer |
| L2DataCertifier | 1 | Data validation mode |

## Not yet — the engine is not ready for paper trading. Here's the evidence.

I checked the actual runtime/broker code and the project's own roadmap doc rather than taking "ready" at face value.

### What's genuinely solid
- **68-parameter Revision 2 registry** (`canonical_parameter_registry.py`) — 45 calibratable + 23 fixed, plus a separate 20-item hard safety layer — validated, tests passing.
- **Fail-closed mode contract** ([runtime/operating_mode.py](runtime/operating_mode.py)) — `OperatingMode.PAPER` exists and `StartupGate` correctly refuses to certify paper mode unless a `PaperBrokerAdapter` is attached and `live_trading_enabled=False`. This is real, well-designed gating logic.
- **One OOS backtest run passed**: `oos_backtest_report.json` shows Sharpe 1.4, max drawdown 0.18, 48-symbol universe, non-synthetic data — but only **5 iterations**, which is thin for regime robustness, and it's an untracked file (not a certified/frozen result).

### What's actually blocking paper trading
1. **`PaperBrokerAdapter` is an empty stub** — `class PaperBrokerAdapter(BrokerAdapter): environment = "paper"`, nothing else. No order state machine, no fill simulation, no PnL tracking against live or replayed ticks.
2. **The project's own roadmap admits this** — [PAPER_EXECUTION_LIFECYCLE_ROADMAP_20260830.md](PAPER_EXECUTION_LIFECYCLE_ROADMAP_20260830.md) states plainly: *"MainTradingLoop is simulation only... does NOT place any orders, track position state, match fills, handle slippage, reconcile with broker."* It scopes the paper-execution layer as **Weeks 1–3 of future work**, not done.
3. **No live/streaming data feed wired into the runtime** — I found no KiteTicker/websocket integration feeding `ecs_runtime_v2.py`. `production_trading_engine.py` only replays static historical CSVs for one symbol at a time — that's a backtest harness, not a live/paper loop.
4. **A currently failing test**: `tests/test_oos_runner.py::test_runner_creates_report_and_returns_summary` fails right now — the OOS runner raises an unhandled `ValueError` on missing data instead of degrading gracefully, which is exactly the kind of edge case that would bite during live paper operation.

### Bottom line
The **safety and parameter-governance layer** is genuinely ready. The **execution layer that paper trading actually depends on — a real paper broker with fills/PnL and a live data feed — does not exist yet**, only a stub and a roadmap.

### Concrete next steps before you can start
1. Implement `PaperBrokerAdapter` for real: order state machine (`PENDING/PARTIAL/FILLED/REJECTED`), fill simulation against ticks, PnL tracking.
2. Wire a live or delayed data feed into `ecs_runtime_v2.py` for at least one symbol.
3. Fix the failing OOS test so the fail-closed path degrades gracefully instead of throwing.
4. Run one single-symbol, market-hours integration test end-to-end in `PAPER` mode (the roadmap suggests SUNPHARMA) and confirm `StartupGate`/`ExecutionGate` behave correctly with a real order flowing through.
5. Only then scale to the 48-symbol universe.

If you want, I can start on #1 (the paper broker) now — that's the actual long pole.

### 1. Real `PaperBrokerAdapter` ([runtime/operating_mode.py](runtime/operating_mode.py))
Replaced the empty stub with a real order lifecycle: `PaperOrder` dataclass (`PENDING → FILLED/REJECTED`), `place_order()` that runs the same `ExecutionGate` safety contract a live broker would, simulates a fill with slippage against a supplied market price, and tracks per-symbol positions + realized PnL. It rejects (never fills) on kill-switch-off, bad quantity, or a missing/invalid price — verified by test.

### 2. Data feed for one symbol ([market_data_loader.py](market_data_loader.py))
Added `SingleSymbolReplayFeed` — reads SUNPHARMA's real 1-minute CSV off disk and yields bars in order. **I need to be upfront: this is a historical replay, not a live or delayed feed.** I don't have Kite API credentials in this environment, so I didn't fabricate a live/websocket connection — that's still an open item, not something I could honestly claim to have wired.

### 3. OOS test fixed to degrade gracefully ([oos_calibration_engine.py](oos_calibration_engine.py))
`OOSBacktestRunner.run()` now catches a missing/invalid data failure and writes a `HOLD` report instead of raising. While in there I also removed something worth flagging: the old code reported **hardcoded placeholder metrics** (`0.18`, `1.4`, `1.7`, etc.) whenever its own internal gate passed, regardless of what the data actually showed. It now reports the metrics `evaluate_oos_performance()` actually computes from real prices.

### 4. Integration test, real data, PAPER mode ([tests/test_paper_execution_integration.py](tests/test_paper_execution_integration.py))
`StartupGate` certifies a PAPER session → a real SUNPHARMA bar → `ExecutionGate` → `PaperBrokerAdapter` fill → position/PnL, plus a kill-switch-block case and an invalid-quantity-reject case. All 4 pass.

---

### The honest limitation — bigger than the broker piece
Building this exposed the real blocker for "run calibration via paper trading": **there is no working strategy signal engine to drive the orders.**

- The 68-parameter Revision 2 registry (`canonical_parameter_registry.py`) only labels which black box (PA/ID/MPC/...) *owns* each parameter — none of those black boxes have actual signal-generation code wired to them.
- I did find a real, older signal engine — `CompleteIntegratedTradingSystem` / `UnifiedP01DGovernor` in [COMPLETE_TRADING_SYSTEM_INTEGRATED_20260829.py](COMPLETE_TRADING_SYSTEM_INTEGRATED_20260829.py) — and ran it against real SUNPHARMA data (30,000 real 1-minute bars). **It produced zero trades.** I haven't root-caused why (likely an entry-gate/threshold bug), and didn't touch its logic since guessing at "correct" thresholds without your sign-off is exactly the kind of thing that caused problems earlier in this project's history.

So the order/fill/PnL plumbing you asked for is now real and tested. What's still missing before a genuine 48-symbol calibration run means anything: either fix why the existing strategy engine never fires, or decide it should be rebuilt against the 68-parameter registry. That's a separate, substantial piece of work — want me to dig into why `UnifiedP01DGovernor` produces zero trades next?

## user

I independently diagnosed the zero-trade behavior using the real SUNPHARMA CSV and 30,000 one-minute bars. No files were modified.
Confirmed rejection funnel
From 29,949 eligible bars:
Condition	Bars passing
Price-change threshold	27,626
Volume-change threshold	27,690
Price/volume phase alignment	15,375
PA quality ≥ 0.60	16
Complete synchronization gate	10
Synchronization + ID filter	7
Entry PID ready	0
Final entries	0
The immediate cause is therefore confirmed: the entry PID never approves a single trade.
Root cause 1 — PID integral windup
The PID calculates:
error = target - current_value
self.integral += error
adjustment = kp * error + ki * integral + kd * derivative
Entry requires:
pid_ready = adjustment < 0
References:
- [COMPLETE_TRADING_SYSTEM_INTEGRATED_20260829.py](/home/shrinivas/ECS_Project/COMPLETE_TRADING_SYSTEM_INTEGRATED_20260829.py)
- [COMPLETE_TRADING_SYSTEM_INTEGRATED_20260829.py](/home/shrinivas/ECS_Project/COMPLETE_TRADING_SYSTEM_INTEGRATED_20260829.py)
Measured after the replay:
PID final integral: 15045.6683
PID adjustment median: 75.2187
PID adjustment maximum: 150.5477
PID-ready bars: 0
The signal exceeded the 0.50 target occasionally—its maximum was 0.7277—but accumulated positive integral error dominated every later decision. The PID resets only after a completed trade, which can never happen because the PID prevents the first entry.
Proper solution
Do not simply lower the entry threshold. Implement:
- bounded integral window,
- integral clamp,
- anti-windup,
- reset on regime/session boundaries,
- direct signal-threshold eligibility separated from PID adjustment,
- canonical pid_integral_window_bars,
- canonical pid_integral_max_clamp,
- canonical pid_derivative_smoothing.
A safer decision structure would be conceptually:
signal_ready = total_signal >= entry_confidence_threshold
pid_adjustment = bounded_pid.update(total_signal)
pid_ready = signal_ready and pid_adjustment_within_limit
The PID should adjust timing or sizing; it should not become a permanently locked binary gate.
Root cause 2 — PA quality is extremely restrictive
Only 16 of 29,949 bars passed:
pa_score >= 0.60
Measured PA distribution:
Median: 0.3351
95th percentile: 0.4868
99th percentile: 0.5261
Maximum: 0.7101
Reference: [COMPLETE_TRADING_SYSTEM_INTEGRATED_20260829.py](/home/shrinivas/ECS_Project/COMPLETE_TRADING_SYSTEM_INTEGRATED_20260829.py)
This is not the final zero-trade cause because seven bars still passed synchronization and ID. Nevertheless, it makes signals extremely sparse.
Solution
Before changing 0.60:
- calculate distributions separately for train and validation;
- verify PA score calibration across all 48 symbols;
- connect the canonical entry_confidence_threshold;
- test whether the PA score represents a calibrated probability or merely a weighted heuristic;
- establish a minimum trade-frequency constraint.
Root cause 3 — This engine is not the 68-parameter Revision 2 engine
CompleteIntegratedTradingSystem passes only six parameters into the governor:
- profit-target ATR multiplier,
- stop-loss ATR multiplier,
- entry PID kp,
- exit PID kp,
- minimum hold bars,
- maximum hold bars.
Reference: [COMPLETE_TRADING_SYSTEM_INTEGRATED_20260829.py](/home/shrinivas/ECS_Project/COMPLETE_TRADING_SYSTEM_INTEGRATED_20260829.py)
Its PA weights and several thresholds are hardcoded. It is therefore an older strategy prototype, not an implementation consuming the canonical 45 calibratable parameters.
Solution
Reuse the working concepts, but adapt them behind Revision 2 interfaces:
- PA consumes all canonical PA parameters.
- ID consumes its canonical parameters.
- MPC consumes ATR targets, stops, PID values and holding rules.
- PositionManager consumes sizing/exposure parameters.
- P01D consumes order and execution parameters.
- Safety gates consume only the frozen safety contract.
Root cause 4 — Initialization contains future-data leakage
Synchronization thresholds and starting parameters are calculated from:
df_history.tail(50)
The system receives the complete dataframe before the backtest begins. It therefore uses the final 50 bars of the period to initialize a simulation that starts thousands of bars earlier.
References:
- [COMPLETE_TRADING_SYSTEM_INTEGRATED_20260829.py](/home/shrinivas/ECS_Project/COMPLETE_TRADING_SYSTEM_INTEGRATED_20260829.py)
- [COMPLETE_TRADING_SYSTEM_INTEGRATED_20260829.py](/home/shrinivas/ECS_Project/COMPLETE_TRADING_SYSTEM_INTEGRATED_20260829.py)
Solution
- Initialize strictly from the warm-up window preceding train.
- During walk-forward runs, recompute only from data available before each fold.
- Never initialize from tail() of the complete train/validation/test dataframe.
- Seal the test window before calibration.
Correct decision
The zero-trade result is not evidence that SUNPHARMA has no signals. It is primarily a control-logic defect:
Seven bars passed synchronization and ID
→ zero bars passed PID
→ zero entries
The correct next work is:
1. Add rejection-funnel tests as permanent diagnostics.
2. Implement bounded PID state using the canonical PID parameters.
3. Remove future-data leakage from initialization.
4. Wire the older PA/ID/MPC concepts to the canonical registry.
5. Verify nonzero trades on SUNPHARMA train data.
6. Confirm validation performance without retuning.
7. Connect successful signals to PaperBrokerAdapter.
8. Integrate all 18 safety gates.
9. Expand chronologically to the 48-symbol portfolio.
10. Only then begin parameter calibration.
So yes—the statement you received identified the right broader blocker. The specific zero-trade root cause is now known: entry PID integral windup is the final blocking gate, with an unusually restrictive PA threshold as a secondary limitation.

## user

The 68-parameter Revision 2 registry (canonical_parameter_registry.py) only labels which black box (PA/ID/MPC/...) owns each parameter — none of those black boxes have actual signal-generation code wired to them.
How to overcome this problem?
4:52 PM
The solution is to turn the registry from a naming catalogue into an executable dependency contract. Each black box must receive its owned parameters, perform a real calculation, and return a typed output consumed by the next box.
Do not wire all 68 parameters directly into one large trading function. Build the execution path box by box.
Target architecture

```
Historical bars
    ↓
DataIngestion
    ↓
L2DataCertifier
    ↓
PA signal calculation
    ↓
ID signal approval/rejection
    ↓
MPC entry/exit plan
    ↓
SafetyGates
    ↓
PositionManager sizing
    ↓
P01D order generation
    ↓
UnifiedExecution
    ↓
PaperBrokerAdapter
```

Every stage should return an explicit result rather than merely report `READY`.
1. Introduce an immutable effective configuration
Create one validated configuration object for each run:

```
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class EffectiveConfig:
    values: Mapping[str, Any]
    registry_hash: str
    config_hash: str

    def require(self, name: str) -> Any:
        if name not in self.values:
            raise MissingParameterError(name)
        return self.values[name]
```

Rules:

* Validate all 68 target values before execution.
* Reject unknown or missing values.
* Keep the 20 hardcoded safety values separately immutable.
* Do not use silent defaults inside black boxes.
* Store the configuration hash in every run report.

Silent code such as this should be avoided:

```
config.get("momentum_period", 20)
```

Use:

```
config.require("momentum_calculation_period")
```

This prevents false passes.
2. Define typed input/output contracts for every box
Example objects:

```
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class MarketSnapshot:
    symbol: str
    timestamp: datetime
    bars: pd.DataFrame

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class PASignal:
    symbol: str
    timestamp: datetime
    direction: int
    confidence: float
    momentum: float
    volatility: float
    vwap_deviation: float

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class IDDecision:
    approved: bool
    reason: str
    confidence: float
    risk_reward_ratio: float

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class TradePlan:
    side: str
    entry_price: float
    stop_price: float
    target_price: float
    minimum_hold_bars: int
    maximum_hold_bars: int
```

Without typed boundaries, black boxes will continue existing only as registry labels.
3. Implement PA as the actual signal generator
PA should calculate:

* momentum,
* VWAP,
* ATR/volatility,
* volume confirmation,
* regime-adjusted component scores,
* smoothed signal,
* final confidence and direction.

Its parameters should include the canonical PA-owned values, for example:

```
class PredictiveAnalyticsBox:
    def evaluate(
        self,
        snapshot: MarketSnapshot,
        config: EffectiveConfig,
    ) -> PASignal:
        momentum_period = config.require("momentum_calculation_period")
        vwap_period = config.require("vwap_calculation_period")
        atr_period = config.require("atr_calculation_period")

        momentum_weight = config.require("momentum_weight")
        vwap_weight = config.require("vwap_weight")
        volatility_weight = config.require("volatility_weight")
        confirmation_weight = config.require("confirmation_2bar_weight")
```

Important requirements:

* Calculate signal from completed bar `t`.
* Do not fill at that same closing price.
* Return a decision intended for bar `t+1`.
* Normalize component values before applying weights.
* Define whether weights must sum to one.
* Use warm-up data without counting warm-up trades.

The older `PredictiveAnalyticsEngine` can be reused, but its hardcoded weights and thresholds must be replaced with canonical configuration reads.
4. Implement ID as a real discriminator
ID should not duplicate PA. It should judge whether the PA signal is tradable.
Example:

```
class IntelligentDiscriminationBox:
    def evaluate(
        self,
        signal: PASignal,
        market: MarketSnapshot,
        config: EffectiveConfig,
    ) -> IDDecision:
        entry_threshold = config.require(
            "entry_confidence_threshold"
        )
        slippage_limit = config.require(
            "slippage_guard_threshold"
        )
        minimum_rr = config.require(
            "min_risk_reward_ratio"
        )
```

ID should evaluate:

* minimum signal confidence,
* direction consistency,
* volatility regime,
* persistence,
* estimated slippage,
* expected reward versus risk,
* rejection reasons.

Every rejected signal should identify the exact condition that failed. This creates the rejection funnel needed to diagnose zero-trade candidates.
5. Implement MPC using correct units
MPC should convert an approved signal into an executable trade plan.
ATR multipliers must be used as multipliers—not percentages:

```
atr = calculate_atr(bars, atr_period)

stop_distance = atr * stop_loss_atr_mult
target_distance = atr * profit_target_atr_mult

if side == "BUY":
    stop_price = entry_price - stop_distance
    target_price = entry_price + target_distance
else:
    stop_price = entry_price + stop_distance
    target_price = entry_price - target_distance
```

MPC should also consume:

* profit-margin buffer,
* minimum absolute profit,
* minimum/maximum holding bars,
* entry/exit PID gains,
* PID integral window,
* integral clamp,
* derivative smoothing,
* slippage-cost multiplier.

Add anti-windup:

```
integral = sum(recent_errors[-integral_window:])
integral = clip(integral, -integral_clamp, integral_clamp)
```

The PID should adjust timing or confidence; it should not permanently block every entry.
6. Connect the fixed 23 target parameters to operational behavior
The fixed target values are part of the 68-target surface but are not optimizer variables.
They should drive:

* symbol universe,
* exclusions,
* strict data validation,
* trading hours,
* order type,
* retry behavior,
* timeout,
* rebalance frequency,
* concentration limits,
* optimization-stage intensities.

They must remain unchanged across optimizer candidates.
Add this assertion:

```
assert candidate.fixed_values == baseline.fixed_values
```

7. Keep the 20 safety values separate
The safety surface should not be merged into the 68-target candidate dictionary.
Use a separate object:

```
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class SafetyContract:
    kill_switch_enabled: bool
    max_daily_loss_rupees: float
    max_concurrent_positions: int
    max_gross_exposure_fraction: float
    ...
```

Before every candidate run:

```
assert safety_contract.hash == frozen_safety_hash
```

Before every paper order:

```
decision = safety_engine.evaluate(
    state=portfolio_state,
    signal=entry_signal,
    trade_plan=trade_plan,
    order=proposed_order,
    event_time=bar.timestamp,
)

if not decision.passed:
    reject_order(decision)
```

The 18 gates should run at their appropriate lifecycle stages, not all with placeholder zero values.
8. Implement PositionManager
PositionManager must convert a trade plan into a safe quantity.
It should consume:

* capital per trade,
* capital buffer,
* maximum live positions,
* maximum positions per symbol,
* sector exposure,
* symbol concentration,
* lot size,
* allocation mode.

Example:

```
risk_per_share = abs(entry_price - stop_price)
risk_budget = available_equity * capital_per_trade_fraction
quantity = floor(risk_budget / risk_per_share)
quantity = round_to_lot_size(quantity, lot_size)
```

Then apply safety derating and portfolio caps.
9. Implement P01D order generation
P01D should translate an approved, sized plan into an order:

```
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ProposedOrder:
    symbol: str
    side: str
    quantity: int
    order_type: str
    limit_price: float | None
    timeout_seconds: int
    max_retries: int
```

It must support:

* MARKET and LIMIT semantics,
* order timeout,
* retry delay,
* deduplication,
* slippage tolerance,
* reconciliation.

Only after P01D and safety approval should the order reach `PaperBrokerAdapter`.
10. Replace runtime acknowledgement with actual execution
Current runtime behavior effectively says:

```
if parameter_name in config:
    consumed = True
```

Replace that with real box calls:

```
pa_result = pa.evaluate(snapshot, config)
id_result = discriminator.evaluate(pa_result, snapshot, config)
trade_plan = mpc.build_plan(id_result, snapshot, config)
sizing = position_manager.size(trade_plan, portfolio, config)
safety = gates.evaluate(...)
order = p01d.create_order(...)
fill = paper_broker.submit(order, next_bar)
```

`READY` should mean:

* required parameters were validated,
* box calculation executed,
* typed output was produced,
* output passed invariants.

11. Add real consumption tracing
Each box should return its parameter-use trace:

```
@dataclass
class ParameterUse:
    parameter: str
    owner: str
    value: Any
    calculation: str
    output_field: str
```

At the end of a run, verify:

```
assert target_parameters_used == all_68_target_parameters
assert calibratable_parameters_used == approved_45
assert safety_parameters_used == frozen_20
```

However, presence in a trace is still not sufficient. Behavioral sensitivity must also be tested.
12. Add behavioral sensitivity tests
For every calibratable parameter:

1. Run with its minimum value.
2. Run with its default value.
3. Run with its maximum value.
4. Hold everything else constant.
5. Compare the owning box’s output.

The test should fail if all outputs are identical:

```
assert min_output != default_output or \
       default_output != max_output
```

The affected output need not always be final P&L. It can be:

* indicator value,
* confidence,
* approval decision,
* stop distance,
* target distance,
* quantity,
* timing,
* execution cost.

This is how to prove all 45 parameters are genuinely active.
13. Adapt the older strategy instead of blindly rebuilding
The existing older engine contains reusable ideas:

* PA score,
* synchronization,
* ID filtering,
* ATR-scaled targets and stops,
* PID timing,
* economic validation.

Recommended approach:

* Extract these calculations into the new box interfaces.
* Replace hardcoded constants with canonical parameter reads.
* Correct PID windup.
* Remove future-data leakage.
* Add next-bar execution.
* Connect the result to `PaperBrokerAdapter`.

Avoid running the old engine and new registry as two independent systems. That would create conflicting sources of truth.
14. Validate one symbol before scaling
Use SUNPHARMA in this order:

1. Warm-up indicators.
2. Run PA on train bars.
3. Count PA-qualified signals.
4. Count ID approvals.
5. Count MPC plans.
6. Count safety approvals.
7. Count submitted orders.
8. Count fills.
9. Count exits.
10. Reconcile cash, positions and net P&L.

Required report:

```
bars processed
PA signals
ID approvals/rejections
MPC plans
gate approvals/rejections
orders submitted
partial/full fills
completed trades
gross P&L
costs
net P&L
ending equity
parameter-consumption coverage
```

Do not optimize until this produces plausible, reproducible trades.
15. Then scale to 48 symbols
After the one-symbol path passes:

* merge all symbol bars chronologically;
* maintain one shared portfolio state;
* apply concurrent-position and exposure gates globally;
* prevent independent per-symbol capital duplication;
* score one portfolio equity curve;
* use train only for candidate fitting;
* use validation for selection;
* freeze the winner;
* open the test period once.

Definition of completion
The wiring problem is resolved only when all of these are true:

* All 68 target parameters have an executable owner.
* All 45 calibratable parameters pass behavioral sensitivity tests.
* All 23 fixed target parameters remain unchanged.
* All 20 safety values remain immutable and execute.
* The 18 gates are integrated into the paper-order lifecycle.
* Signals produce reproducible nonzero trades.
* Trades use next-bar executable prices.
* P&L is net of costs.
* One-symbol integration passes.
* 48-symbol chronological portfolio replay passes.
* Train, validation and test remain isolated.

The best practical approach is therefore: adapt the older PA/ID/MPC calculations into typed Revision 2 boxes, wire them through one orchestrator, prove parameter sensitivity, and only then connect calibration.

## user

Approach this as the design lead at a small studio known for their versatility, giving every client a visual identity pitched at the treatment the task actually calls for. Make deliberate choices about palette, typography, and layout that are specific to this subject, and avoid templated designs.

## Read the request first

Calibrate treatment, not whether to design. A doc deserves the same craft as a landing page - what changes is the treatment that craft is delivered in. Format is not part of this read: author HTML, and publish Markdown only when a loaded skill explicitly instructs it - a Markdown publish keeps its filename as its title and takes almost none of the craft below, and is never a way to save time.

Many requests call for a more utilitarian treatment: a plan, a memo, a demo. Make it polished: include real typographic hierarchy, considered spacing, and a proper palette, but avoid over-designing. Most pages do not need a flashy, gigantic hero. Keep flourishes tasteful and limited.

Some requests call for an editorial treatment: a landing page, a game, an app or tool they'll keep or share.

When unsure: a well-composed page is never the wrong answer; an over-designed visual identity sometimes is.

Fundamentals below apply to everything. The editorial process after that runs only when the read above says so.

## Fundamentals for every artifact

**Honor what's already there** Look for an existing design system first - CLAUDE.md, a tokens or theme file, existing component styles. When one exists, apply it; everything below fills gaps and never overrides. Precedence is always: the user's own words, then the project's existing system, then your choices.

**Ground it in the subject.** If the subject isn't already clear, pin it: one concrete subject, its audience, and the page's single job. The subject's own world - its materials, instruments, vernacular - is where distinctive choices come from. Whatever the treatment, carry at least one detail only this subject would have - its real units and scales, its document conventions, its terms of art - as content, not ornament; it costs a plain page nothing. Build with real content throughout, never lorem.

**Pair typefaces** Typography carries the page even when the page isn't about typography. Google Fonts is the one font host the Artifact CSP admits - link it directly (`<link rel="stylesheet" href="https://fonts.googleapis.com/css2?family=...&display=swap">`); a face from anywhere else must be inlined as a @font-face data URI or it falls back silently. Either way, declare a real fallback stack. Keep running text near 65 characters wide; set a type scale and stay on it; give headings `text-wrap: balance`, body text room to breathe, and uppercase labels a touch of letter-spacing.

**Load libraries, don't paste them.** When the page genuinely needs a library - React, a charting or highlighting package - load its UMD build from cdnjs (only the script - a library's stylesheet still has to be inlined) with one pinned `<script src="https://cdnjs.cloudflare.com/ajax/libs/...">` placed before the inline script that uses its global, instead of inlining the library's source or hand-writing a stand-in; the Artifact tool's description lists the few other script hosts the CSP admits. The page's own CSS and JS, its images and its data ship with the page. Most pages need no library at all - reach for one only when it carries real weight.

**Choose neutrals, don't default to them.** A pure mid-grey reads as unconsidered; a grey with a slight hue bias toward the page's accent reads as chosen. Pure white and near-black are fine grounds when they suit the subject - the point is that the neutral was picked, not inherited.

**Design both themes.** The page renders in the viewer's theme, and the viewer has three states, not two: an explicit choice stamps `data-theme="dark"` / `data-theme="light"` on the root element, and the default "system" setting stamps *nothing* - most viewers see the un-stamped document, where only `prefers-color-scheme` separates light from dark. Structure the CSS token-level for all three: the bare `:root` block defines the complete light palette (for a deliberately dark-first design, swap light and dark consistently through this whole pattern); `@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark)` redefines only the tokens, guarded as `:root:not([data-theme="light"])` so an explicit light choice beats a dark OS; `:root[data-theme="dark"]` redefines them again so the toggle also wins in the other direction. Style components through the tokens, never directly inside a media or `[data-theme]` block - a color whose only definition sits behind `[data-theme]` never applies in the un-stamped state, and the page renders one theme's text on the other theme's ground. Two more rules keep each theme resolving as a set: the artifact composites over a ground the viewer paints in *its* theme, so `body` must set an explicit `background` from a token - a transparent body silently borrows the host's ground; and every element that sets a color takes it from the same token set as the surface behind it, never a literal that only works in one theme. Declare every token in the bare `:root` block before any media or `[data-theme]` block redefines it - a color that exists only inside one of those blocks is the classic unreadable-artifact bug. Give the second theme the same care as the first - don't naively invert; keep contrast legible and the accent working on both grounds. A design that deliberately commits to one visual world (a neon arcade screen, a letterpress invitation) may stay single-theme - then skip the media query and stamps entirely but still paint the background and every color explicitly, so the page holds on either host ground; make it a choice, not an omission.

**Let layout do the spacing.** Lay out sibling groups with flex or grid and `gap`, not per-element margins that silently collapse or double. Wide content - tables, code, diagrams - gets `overflow-x: auto` on its own container so the page body never scrolls sideways. Reach for `font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums` wherever digits line up in columns.

**Compose repeated things as one object.** Cards in a row, label/value pairs down a list, badges on siblings: same edges, baselines and inner padding from one to the next, and a recurring element sits in the same place on each. Let content set a container's height and pick a column count the items fill, so nothing stretches over dead space or sits alone in a row. Text that can outgrow its track wraps or scrolls in its own container; clipped text is a bug.

**Not everything is a card.** Border, fill, radius and shadow each say "separate object" - spend them by role, lifting the one thing that needs it, instead of one radius and one shadow stamped on every block, which flattens the hierarchy. Lead with big-number tiles only when those figures are the point of the page.

**Draw charts to the scale.** One scale places marks, ticks and labels, and every label names a value the chart reaches; chart text takes its color from the theme tokens so it reads in both themes; marks, labels and edges stay clear of one another and inside the drawing's bounds - in SVG, leave room in the viewBox for the outermost labels and give every drawn shape an explicit fill.

**Show the page at rest.** Everything meant to be read is visible once the page has loaded, without scrolling to trigger it - that first still frame is what a thumbnail, a shared link, and a skimming reader all get. A section may animate in, but from a visible resting state, never parked at `opacity: 0` waiting on an observer. Size a hero to what it holds, not to the viewport; a `100vh` opener pushes the page itself out of that first frame. A tool or app opens in a realistic working state - the user's real data where it exists, otherwise example rows, a loaded sample, a form someone plausibly filled, plainly marked as examples and never passed off as the user's own figures - so the first look shows what it does; an empty shell waiting for input shows nothing.

**Avoid AI-generated design** AI-generated design currently clusters around a few looks: warm cream (#F4F1EA) with a serif display and terracotta accent; near-black with a lone acid-green or vermilion pop; broadsheet hairline rules with dense columns; a purple-to-blue gradient hero on white; Inter or Space Grotesk as the "safe" face; emoji as section markers; everything centered; `rounded-lg` everywhere; accent bar/rail on rounded cards. Where the user pins down a visual direction, follow it exactly - their words always win, including when they ask for one of these looks. Where nothing is specified, don't spend that freedom on one of these defaults.

**Build cleanly** Be cognizant of overlapping elements, cascade collisions, silent font fallbacks. Close every non-void element, double-quote attributes, give keyboard focus a visible state, respect `prefers-reduced-motion`. For generative or decorative graphics, reach for Canvas or WebGL rather than hand-authoring long SVG path data.

**CSS rules** When writing the CSS, watch your selector specificities. It is easy to generate classes that cancel each other out - a type-based selector like `.section` fighting an element-based one like `.cta` over padding and margins between sections. Structure the cascade so it doesn't silently undo your spacing.

**Writing the copy** Words are design material, not decoration. Write from the user's side of the screen - name things by what people recognize, not how the system is built (a person manages *notifications*, not *webhook config*). Active voice; a control says exactly what happens ("Publish", then a toast that says "Published"). Errors explain what went wrong and how to fix it - no apologies, no vagueness. Specific beats clever.

**Name the page like a product, not a caption.** The `<title>` is the artifact's name in the gallery and the browser tab, and it sets the reader's first impression of care. Give the page a real name: a short noun phrase, typically two to four words, specific to the subject - or, for a page that exists to answer one question, that question itself, which is then the page's name. Stop at the name - a title that carries its own explainer after a dash or colon reads as generated filler. The name must also identify the page among many: in the gallery it sits beside dozens of other artifacts, and a generic category label that could sit on any of them fails as a name just as surely as an appended explainer. When a candidate title pairs the name with a generic word - a greeting, a category, a page-type label - the name is the half to keep; a trim that drops the identity and keeps the generic word produces exactly the title that could sit on any page. And the rule removes explainers, it does not impose brevity: a multi-word title that already reads as one specific name is finished, and shortening it further only makes it generic. The one-sentence publish `description` is where the explanation belongs; the gallery shows it right under the title.

**Structure is information** Structural devices, numbering, eyebrows, dividers, labels, should encode something true about the content, not decorate it. Many generic designs use numbered markers (01 / 02 / 03), but that's only appropriate if the content actually is a sequence - like a real process or a typed timeline where order carries information the reader needs. Question if choices like numbered markers actually make sense before incorporating them.

**When it's a UI, not a document** A dashboard or tool is scanned and operated, not read top-to-bottom, so the craft shifts from typography to information design. Surface the summary before the detail; encode state in form as well as number - a pill, a chip, a severity stripe - so what needs attention reads at a glance. Semantic color (good / warning / critical) is separate from the accent hue and doesn't count as your accent. Give sparklines and charts the same care as type: an area fill, a faint grid, an emphasized endpoint. What's interactive should look interactive.



## Process

Before writing code, sketch a short design plan - a compact token system with color, type, and layout:
- **Color**: describe the palette as 4-6 named hex values.
- **Type**: typefaces for 2+ roles - a characterful display face used with restraint, a complementary body face, and a utility face for captions or data if needed.
- **Layout**: a layout concept in one or two sentences.

Then build, following the plan and deriving every color and type decision from it.

**Write, look once, publish.** Before publishing you may look at the rendered page once - one screenshot of the local file, or the Artifact tool's preview where it offers one - then one pass of edits for what it shows, without a second look. For a page that charts real numbers, take that look rather than skip it, and spend it on the chart. Don't build a test loop around your own file: no repeated screenshots, no pulling the script out to run it through node, no scripts that probe the DOM. That loop spends the session re-checking what a careful write already settled, while the user waits for a link. Then publish, check once any `window.claude` call the preview couldn't run, and stop: the live page is the review surface, and further polish is the user's to ask for. If the user reports something visibly broken - a clipped column, unreadable text, a control that does nothing - fix that and republish once.

## When the request is editorial

The stance shifts: the client has already rejected proposals that felt templated, and is paying for a distinctive point of view. Make opinionated calls, and take one real aesthetic risk where it serves the work.

Review the design plan against the subject before building: if any part of it reads like the generic default you would produce for any similar page, revise that part, and note what you changed and why. Only after you've confirmed the plan's uniqueness do you write the code, following the revised plan exactly.

**Principles** 

- The hero is a thesis: open with the most characteristic thing in the subject's world - headline, image, live demo, interactive moment. 
- Typography carries the personality of the page. Pair the display and body faces deliberately, not the same families you would reach for on any other project, and set a clear type scale with intentional weights, widths, and spacing. Make the type treatment itself a memorable part of the design, not a neutral delivery vehicle for the content. 
- Leverage motion deliberately. Think about where and if animation can serve the subject: a page-load sequence, hover micro-interactions, ambient atmosphere. An orchestrated moment usually lands harder than scattered effects; choose what the direction calls for. However, sometimes less is more, and extra animation contributes to the feeling that the design is AI-generated. 
- Match complexity to the vision. Maximalist directions need elaborate execution; minimal directions need precision in spacing, type, and detail. Elegance is executing the chosen vision well.
- Spend your boldness in one place; keep everything around it quiet. If the accent fights the ground, shift it toward analogous or drop saturation rather than replacing it.

## user

Draw as the engineer who has to live with the decision, not as a decorator: a diagram earns its place when it lets a cold reader see a mechanism they would otherwise have to assemble from prose - where data flows, which components talk, what changes between two options, what state a request moves through. If a sentence says it faster, write the sentence.

## What to draw

**Depict the mechanism, not its name.** A box labeled "cache" says less than the prose; the path a request takes through it, the two stores it sits between, and the arrow that disappears when the cache is removed say what the words can't. Show the parts that the argument hinges on - the boundary being crossed, the hop being added, the data that moves - and leave out the parts that don't.

**Comparing options?** Draw the difference. Two architectures side by side, a before and an after, the one edge that each option adds or removes - the reader should be able to point at what they are choosing between. A separate labeled box per option, with nothing connecting them to the system, is not a comparison; it is a restated option list.

**Match complexity to the stakes.** A one-hop question is a three-box diagram; a migration that reroutes writes through a queue needs the queue, the writer, the reader, and the ordering arrow. Draw as much as the decision actually turns on - no forced minimalism, no inventory of the whole system either.

**Label the arrows.** An unlabeled arrow is "related somehow"; `writes`, `invalidates`, `polls every 30s` is information. A legend is only worth it when the same encoding (dashed, colored, doubled) repeats; otherwise put the meaning on the mark itself.

## Inline SVG mechanics

These mechanics apply where the page renders inline SVG natively (HTML pages); a markdown-rendered page draws its diagrams in whatever fence that lane's renderer supports, and the skill that owns the lane says which. Hand-author inline `<svg>` with native shapes (`rect`, `circle`, `line`, `polyline`, `path`) and `<text>` - no libraries, no runtime, no external images.

- **Size by `viewBox`.** Set `viewBox="0 0 W H"` and let CSS scale it (`max-width: 100%; height: auto`); choose W and H for the content, not a preset. Wide flows read left-to-right; layered stacks read top-to-bottom.
- **Theme with `currentColor`.** Strokes, text, and arrowheads in `currentColor` inherit the page's foreground in light and dark themes alike; reserve a literal hue for the one element that carries meaning (the option leaned toward, the hop under discussion), and make sure it reads on both grounds.
- **Arrowheads are markers or polygons.** A `<defs><marker>` referenced by `marker-end="url(#arrow)"` (fragment-internal id) or a small `<polygon>` at the line's end - never an image.
- **Keep text legible.** Roughly 11-13px at the drawn scale, `text-anchor` for alignment, short labels (a word or three); explanatory sentences belong in the caption below the figure, not in the drawing.
- **Align to a grid.** Shared baselines and even gaps are most of what makes a hand diagram read as deliberate; eyeballed offsets read as noise.
- **One figure, one claim.** Wrap the `<svg>` in `<figure>` with a `<figcaption>` that states what the picture shows, and give the `<svg>` `role="img"` plus an `aria-label` carrying the same claim for readers who cannot see it.
- **Stay self-contained.** No `<script>`, `<style>`, or `<foreignObject>` inside the SVG; gradients, patterns, and `<use>` reference ids in the same fragment (`href="#id"`). Long decorative path data is a sign the drawing wants a real graphics tool - simplify instead.

### 1. Black-box I/O audit — verified against the code, then fixed where it didn't match the logic

| Box | Input it actually receives | Output | Matched the logic? |
|---|---|---|---|
| **StartupCapabilityLock** | *(nothing — never called)* | — | **No.** Existed in `runtime/operating_mode.py` but was never invoked by the orchestrator. Still open — noted below. |
| **DataIngestion** | `symbol` + config (no bar data) | admit/reject | Was checking universe membership only, not bar data — acceptable for a single-symbol run, real gap at 48-symbol scale (documented). |
| **L2DataCertifier** | last 5 OHLCV bars | certified/reject | Validates bar sanity, not real L2 order-book depth (none exists in this dataset) — labeled honestly in the commit. |
| **PA** | bar-`t`-only `MarketSnapshot` | `PASignal` | Matched. |
| **ID** | `PASignal` only | `IDDecision` | Matched — correctly doesn't recompute PA. |
| **MPC** | signal + decision + entry price + ATR | `TradePlan` | **Fixed**: now enforces `min_risk_reward_ratio` on the target and uses a properly-scaled `minimum_absolute_profit_rupees` check — both were read but inert before. |
| **SafetyGates (target)** | 2-point equity snapshot + plan | approve/size-mult | **Fixed**: was comparing a per-share price distance to a rupee cap, and running before quantity existed. Split into `evaluate_pre_sizing` (drawdown/halt/lambda) and `evaluate_post_sizing` (rupee-denominated, after quantity is known). |
| **PositionManager** | plan + equity + size-mult | quantity | Matched, with sector exposure honestly documented as a single-symbol surrogate. |
| **P01D** | plan + quantity | `ProposedOrder` | Matched for order construction; retry/dedup/reconciliation still unimplemented as behavior (open item). |
| **UnifiedExecution** | timestamp | in-window + exploration bias | Runs as a pre-check (trading hours), not the target diagram's final gateway role — structural deviation, documented, not fixed this pass. |
| **18-gate EntryDecisionEngine** | *(nothing — never called)* | — | **Fixed**: `gates_framework.py`'s real 18-gate engine is now wired in as an independent enforcement layer before every order, with its own `SystemState`/`EntrySignal`, tracked as `gates_evaluated/passed/rejected`. |
| **PaperBrokerAdapter** | order + market price | fill/PnL | Matched — plus the exit-priority bug (below). |

### 2. Correctness fixes — all verified against the code before fixing, all now covered by regression tests
- **Exact file matching**: `LT` could resolve to `...ULTRACEMCO...` via substring wildcard. Fixed to exact-filename-first; verified all 48 symbols resolve correctly.
- **`validate_market_universe`** had drifted onto `SingleSymbolReplayFeed` (would `AttributeError` on any real multi-symbol run) — moved back to `MarketDataLoader`.
- **Exception masking**: `OOSBacktestRunner` caught bare `Exception`; narrowed to `FileNotFoundError`/`ValueError` only — a real bug now propagates instead of becoming a calm HOLD.
- **Stop-priority bug**: a protective stop could be suppressed by `minimum_hold_bars` — the same class of bug that caused the original zero-trade PID lockup. Fixed: forced-close > stop > target > signal-exit (the only one gated by min-hold, and now genuinely wired to PA's `exit_confidence`) > max-hold.
- **Quantity-aware risk check**: per-trade rupee cap now checked after sizing, in rupees.
- **End-of-run reconciliation**: an open position is now force-closed and counted, never left out of `net_pnl`.
- **Cost accounting**: added `slippage_cost`/`brokerage`/`exchange_charges`/`taxes`/`total_cost`, `net_pnl` now genuinely differs from `gross_pnl`.
- **Deep immutability**: `EffectiveConfig`/`SafetyContract` now reject item assignment (`MappingProxyType` + tuple-ized lists).

### 3. Checkpoint (Stage 1)
Committed on `codex/revision2-executable-pipeline` (not pushed) — scoped to exactly the milestone files, limitations spelled out in the commit message, everything else in your working tree untouched.

### 4. The DCS console
**[Revision 2 Operating Station →](https://claude.ai/code/artifact/22d222a6-340d-4e4b-85e5-ddfee1167ac6)** — replays the corrected orchestrator's real recorded run against 6,000 real SUNPHARMA bars: 13 live faceplates lighting up bar-by-bar exactly as far as that bar actually got through the pipeline, the exit-priority chain, an equity trend, a rejection funnel, and an event log of every real fill/exit/gate-rejection. Play/pause/scrub/speed controls, clearly labeled as a replay of a recorded run, not a live feed.

### Still open (Stages 3–7, unaddressed by design)
Golden-run certification, the 48-symbol shared-portfolio merge, train/validation/test sealing, and calibration itself — none of this is calibrated or validated yet, just correctly wired and reproducible.

## Important finding while building your requested benchmark: a real O(n²) scaling bug, now fixed

Before I even got clean numbers, I caught something your caution about "measure before committing to a full 3-year run" was specifically designed to catch.

### The bug
Both orchestrators built every bar's `MarketSnapshot` as `bars.iloc[:bar_idx + 1]` — the **entire history so far**, not a bounded lookback. PA converts that whole slice to numpy before using only its last ~30 bars. On a 600-bar smoke test this is invisible. At real scale (~291,000 bars/symbol), it means total run cost scales **quadratically**, not linearly — a naive linear extrapolation from short-window timing would have been badly wrong, possibly by an order of magnitude, exactly the risk you flagged.

### The fix
Bounded the snapshot to a trailing 300-bar window (`SNAPSHOT_LOOKBACK_BARS`, generous headroom above any registry lookback max) in both orchestrators. Proven, not just applied:
- **Correctness**: PA's output is byte-identical (10 decimal places) bounded vs. full-history on the same bar.
- **Performance**: per-bar cost measured flat from bar_idx=500 to bar_idx=29,000 (would have blown past a 4× tolerance under the old code).

Committed (`72a0e56`), full regression suite unaffected.

### Also done this pass
**`revision2/dataset_manifest.py`** (Stage E item 1) — SHA-256 hashes all 48 real CSVs, records row/timestamp bounds, verifies later against the frozen record. Built and verified for real: **48/48 files, byte-identical**. The manifest itself is committed (`revision2/DATASET_MANIFEST_48SYMBOL_1MIN.json`) since — unlike the trace JSON — this one's whole purpose is to be a checked-in, versioned reference.

### One more thing to flag, not fix
A `scripts/calibration_watchdog.py` appeared in the working tree during this session that **I did not create**. It references `CalibrationSupervisor` and `output/calibration_checkpoint.json` directly — it's clearly aware of what I built here, which means there's concurrent agent activity in this repo (the `codex/` branch name you suggested earlier now makes sense in that light). I left it and `output/` completely alone and didn't commit either. Worth knowing before two agents step on each other's work.

### Now running
The corrected, linear-cost benchmark (600 vs 15,000 bars, ~25× apart) is running in the background to give you a real per-bar-event rate to extrapolate the 3-year figure from. I'll report the numbers as soon as it completes.

## Both reviews checked, most-critical item fixed, honest status on the rest

Your #3 and #9 (same finding, independently rediscovered in your follow-up) was the most important thing in either review, and I'd already started on it: **TPE only learned from finite scores, and the supervisor required an *accepted* phase-1 candidate before it would even start phase 2/3.** That's exactly what happened on the real 48-symbol run — everything got rejected, so it silently stopped after phase 1 and reported "done" with nothing learned. Fixed with the two-value split you and I both converged on independently: `feasible` (hard boolean, unchanged) + `guidance_score` (always finite, what the search actually optimizes). Committed (`abf4bab`), full affected-suite tests green (20/20).

Also fixed this pass, each verified against the code first:
- **Net-of-cost trade P&L** (#18) — each trade now carries its own allocated brokerage/exchange/tax and a `net_pnl`; gates/scoring use it, so a candidate can't clear profit-factor/drawdown/expectancy on gross P&L while unprofitable net of costs.
- **Real marked-to-market equity curve** — tracks true chronological equity including unrealized P&L on open positions, additive/parallel to existing logic so no trading decision changed. A real annualized Sharpe now comes from it.
- **`AcceptanceGates.production_defaults()`** — your exact recommended bar, named explicitly and separate from the (now explicitly-named) `smoke_test_defaults()`.
- **Clock caching** — built once per calibration run instead of every candidate rebuilding and re-sorting it.
- **Narrowed exception handling** — expected data problems are recorded as a rejected candidate; anything else is treated as a real bug and aborts after 3 consecutive occurrences.

**Empirical evidence your #1 (clock scaling) is real**: a single-candidate benchmark at 15,000 bars (25× the smoke test) shows per-bar cost up 21.5%, not flat — extrapolating that trend to the full dataset suggests closer to **9–10 hours per candidate**, worse than my earlier naive linear estimate. Caching amortizes this across candidates within one run but doesn't fix the underlying O(N log N) build your streaming-merge suggestion would.

### Honest status on the rest

Given both reviews together now total ~30 distinct findings, I'm not going to attempt the rest superficially in this same pass. Verified-real, not yet touched, in your own priority order: same-timestamp execution causality (#2/loop-#1) — the biggest remaining one; simulated-healthy safety-gate inputs (#4/loop-#7); actual-fill-into-plan feedback (loop-#4); daily-loss session reset (#6/loop-#6); CMA-ES mathematical correction (#8/loop-#10); atomic checkpoint+optimizer-state resume (#9); cash/margin ledger (#13); parameter relational validation (#11); train/validation/test one-way sealing (#6, loop-#12) — this one now matters more given the guidance-score fix, since it's what will actually let a real winner emerge. I did not implement any of these.

Given the volume, I'd rather you pick the next 3–4 to prioritize than have me keep guessing at ordering across two long reviews.

Supporting feedback loops then return:
fills → execution-quality controller
positions/P&L → portfolio risk
market observations → PA state
completed trials → calibration optimizer
That separation between forward decision flow and controlled feedback is correct in principle.
Black-box concept review
2. Startup Capability Lock
Conceptually correct as the system’s initial permissive.
It should answer only:
Is this exact system configuration authorized to start?

It should not participate in signal generation or calibration quality. Its output should be a prerequisite certificate consumed by every run.
Current conceptual weakness: durable persistence and runtime health are partly asserted rather than demonstrated.
3. Data Ingestion
The name suggests it acquires, identifies and normalizes market data. Currently, its implemented responsibility is closer to UniverseAdmission.
Those are separate concepts:
Market Data Source
→ ingestion and normalization
→ universe admission
→ data certification
Recommendation: split or rename it:
- MarketDataIngestion
- UniverseAdmissionGate
This removes ambiguity about what the box guarantees.
4. L2 Data Certifier
Conceptually mismatched with the available data.
The system supplies OHLCV bars—not Level 2 order-book data. Calling this box L2DataCertifier implies bid/ask depth, spread and book-quality validation that does not exist.
Recommendation:
- rename it MarketBarCertifier; or
- provide real L2 snapshots and retain the current name.
A black-box name must accurately represent its information content.
5. Predictive Analytics
This is conceptually well placed.
Its responsibility should remain:
past and current certified observations
→ forecast/bias/confidence/features
It should not know:
- portfolio cash;
- position limits;
- broker state;
- final order quantity;
- future bar prices.
Its output should be an economic forecast, not an execution decision.
Recommended output:
ForecastSignal
- symbol
- observation timestamp
- forecast horizon
- direction
- expected return
- uncertainty
- confidence
- volatility estimate
- regime
- feature provenance
The current PASignal is reasonable but lacks explicit forecast horizon, uncertainty and provenance.
6. Intelligent Discrimination
The concept is valid: prevent every PA forecast from becoming a trade.
However, its current responsibility overlaps MPC. ID evaluates risk/reward before the actual stop and target exist.
A better division is:
ID:
Is the forecast statistically and operationally credible?

MPC:
Can a financially acceptable trade be constructed from it?
ID should check:
- signal confidence;
- stability/persistence;
- regime compatibility;
- liquidity quality;
- data quality;
- prediction uncertainty.
MPC or a later economic gate should check actual reward/risk and expected net profit.
7. Model Predictive Control
MPC is logically the least settled box.
A genuine MPC normally:
- models future system behaviour;
- considers alternative control actions;
- optimizes an objective over a horizon;
- applies the first selected action;
- repeats when new information arrives.
The current implementation mainly converts ATR and confidence into entry, stop and target values, with PID modifiers. That is more accurately a TradePlanController than full MPC.
There are two valid choices:
1. Keep the current simpler implementation and rename it honestly.
2. Implement genuine horizon-based MPC with candidate actions, forecast trajectories, constraints and a defined objective.
The architecture should not depend on an advanced name if the corresponding control mathematics is absent.
8. Position Manager
Its conceptual position is correct—after a provisional trade plan and before final safety authorization.
It should receive:
TradePlan
PortfolioSnapshot
RiskBudget
InstrumentMetadata
It should return:
SizedTradeProposal
The proposal must include:
- symbol\Repositories;
- side;
- proposed quantity;
- notional;
- margin requirement;
- maximum-case loss;
- sector exposure after entry;
- gross exposure after entry;
- available capital after entry.
Currently too much of that information is reconstructed downstream.
9. Safety Gates
Independent safety authorization is conceptually essential. But there are currently overlapping layers:
- SafetyGates target box;
- 18-gate EntryDecisionEngine;
- ExecutionGate;
- PaperBroker validation.
Defence in depth is good, but overlapping responsibilities can produce inconsistent decisions.
The boundaries should be explicit:
Portfolio Risk Gate
- drawdown
- daily loss
- exposure
- concentration
- risk budget

Order Authorization Gate
- symbol
- quantity
- allowed side/type
- cutoff time
- duplication
- kill switch

Execution Validation
- fill price
- slippage
- partial fill
- timeout
- reconciliation
A fill-dependent check such as slippage cannot logically run before a fill using target_price == fill_price.
10. P01D
Conceptually, P01D should be the sovereign order-intent generator:
authorized SizedTradeProposal
→ immutable OrderIntent
It should not claim execution responsibility. Timeout, retries, cancellation and fills belong to the execution lifecycle.
Its output should include:
- immutable client order ID;
- decision timestamp;
- intended execution timestamp;
- configuration hash;
- safety-certificate hash;
- limit/market instructions;
- expiration;
- idempotency key;
- permitted slippage;
- retry policy.
11. Unified Execution
Its present placement and name are ambiguous.
Trading-hours validation currently happens near the beginning, while “Unified Execution” sounds like the final execution layer.
These should be separated:
Session Controller
→ early trading-window decision

Execution Controller
→ final order submission and lifecycle
The optimizer’s exploration controls should not enter either runtime box.
12. Paper Broker
Conceptually correct as an external actuator/simulator.
It should consume an authorized order—not raw strategy intent—and return asynchronous execution events:
ACKNOWLEDGED
PARTIALLY_FILLED
FILLED
REJECTED
EXPIRED
CANCELLED
It should not instantaneously manufacture a completed fill for every accepted order.
Communication between boxes
13. Typed communication is the right approach
Using typed, immutable messages is substantially better than sharing mutable dictionaries.
Every message should carry a common envelope:
event_id
correlation_id
symbol
observation_timestamp
decision_timestamp
effective_timestamp
configuration_hash
dataset/source identity
producer box
schema version
This permits causal auditing from final P&L back to the exact input and configuration.
14. Information should move forward without reinterpretation
Each downstream box should consume an upstream result, not recompute or reinterpret the previous box’s work.
Examples:
- ID should not recreate PA.
- Safety should not reconstruct quantity.
- Broker should not reconstruct order policy.
- Reporting should not reconstruct portfolio equity from an incomplete trade list.
The current architecture still reconstructs some state rather than passing authoritative typed objects.
15. Avoid information loss
Several current outputs contain too little information for downstream decisions.
For example:
PASignal
→ IDDecision
IDDecision carries confidence and a constructed risk/reward ratio, but loses forecast horizon, uncertainty, liquidity assumptions and detailed rejection evidence.
Similarly, TradePlan lacks symbol and expected costs.
The solution is not to pass every internal variable. It is to pass every fact needed to verify the next box’s decision.
Forward bias and causality
16. Feed-forward processing is correct; future-data access is not
Using bar t to generate a decision for bar t+1 is logical:
bar t closes
→ calculate signal
→ authorize order
→ submit pending intent
→ fill at bar t+1 open
What is not logical is immediately updating portfolio state at time t with the bar t+1 fill.
The event model must distinguish:
- observation time;
- decision time;
- submission time;
- effective fill time.
17. Simultaneous symbols need batch processing
At each timestamp:
1. Update all symbols with observations at that timestamp.
2. Evaluate exits.
3. Generate all entry candidates.
4. Rank candidates using a frozen portfolio snapshot.
5. Allocate capital.
6. create pending orders.
7. Execute them only at their eligible future price event.
Otherwise alphabetical processing order becomes an unintended strategy parameter.
18. Calibration information flow must be one-way
The correct calibration architecture is:
Training
  ↕ optimizer feedback permitted

Validation
  → selection only
  → no parameter learning

Test
  → reporting only
  → no feedback whatsoever
That separation is as important as eliminating next-bar price leakage.
Final output
19. The final output should not simply be “BUY” or “SELL”
The authoritative final output should be a complete run ledger:
RunResult
├── frozen run identity
├── dataset and configuration hashes
├── startup certificate
├── candidate/trial history
├── signal decisions
├── rejected-order reasons
├── order lifecycle events
├── fills and reconciliation
├── positions
├── gross and net P&L
├── chronological equity curve
├── safety events
├── risk-adjusted metrics
└── acceptance verdict
For a single trading decision, the final output should be:
AUTHORIZED ORDER
or
REJECTED DECISION with exact responsible gate
No downstream component should be able to convert a rejection back into an order.
Overall opinion
The black-box concept is good. The serial decomposition is appropriate and safer than one monolithic strategy engine. The strongest architectural ideas are:
- explicit ownership;
- typed interfaces;
- immutable parameter contracts;
- independent safety authorization;
- deterministic audit trails;
- separation of calibration from runtime trading.
The design is not yet perfectly logical in four areas:
1. Some box names overstate their implementation—especially L2 and MPC.
2. Responsibilities overlap—especially safety and execution.
3. Information contracts omit important timing, cost, portfolio and provenance fields.
4. Temporal causality is incorrect around next-bar fills and simultaneous symbols.
My recommended canonical architecture is:
Startup Certification
        ↓
Data Ingestion
        ↓
Universe Admission
        ↓
Market Data Certification
        ↓
Session Controller
        ↓
Predictive Analytics
        ↓
Signal Discrimination
        ↓
Trade-Plan Controller
        ↓
Portfolio Allocator
        ↓
Portfolio Risk Authorization
        ↓
Order Intent Generator
        ↓
Execution Controller
        ↓
Broker
        ↓
Fill/Reconciliation Ledger
        ↓
Portfolio State + Reporting
That architecture is conceptually coherent. Calibration then sits outside it, repeatedly providing frozen candidate configurations and observing only the resulting authenticated run reports.

## assistant

This is a coherent target architecture, and it's valuable as a synthesis — but I want to be straight about scope rather than start rewriting box names and interfaces in the tail of an already very long session.

## Assessment: this is a design doc, not a bug list

Most of the earlier reviews (the 22-item and the feedback-loop ones) found concrete defects I could verify against running code and fix in place. This one is different: it's proposing a **rewrite of box names, message contracts, and the temporal execution model**. That's the right target, but implementing it now — live, on top of a large, tested, working system — is a different kind of risk than the fixes so far.

### What's actually new here vs. what you've already told me

Points **16–18** (batch-per-timestamp processing, observe-then-execute-at-future-price, one-way calibration flow) are the **same finding** as your second review's #2 and #12/loop-#1 — same-timestamp execution causality. I already logged that as the single biggest deferred item. This review adds the concrete redesign (steps 1–7 under #17) for how to actually fix it, which is genuinely useful — it's the first message that's given me a full solution shape, not just the diagnosis.

Everything else here (#2–15, #19) is new: naming honesty (L2→MarketBarCertifier, MPC→TradePlanController or real MPC), splitting DataIngestion, three explicit safety tiers instead of four overlapping ones, typed message envelopes with causal-audit fields, and a `RunResult` as the real final output instead of a bare BUY/SELL.

### What I'm doing with it

Not implementing tonight. Renaming boxes and redesigning message contracts touches every file I've built and tested this session (`boxes.py`, both orchestrators, `calibration_supervisor.py`, and ~10 test files) — doing that carefully needs its own focused pass, not a bolt-on at the end of this one. Rushing it now is exactly the kind of thing that turns a good architecture doc into a broken build.

**My recommendation**: treat this message as the design brief for the next dedicated session, in this order, since it subsumes and completes the earlier open items:
1. The causality/batch-processing redesign (#16–17) — this is the one piece of unfinished business from every prior review, now with an actual spec.
2. Message contract/naming honesty (#2–12) — mechanical but wide-reaching; safe to do once #1 isn't also in flux underneath it.
3. `RunResult` as the real final output (#19) — natural once the above two exist.

If you'd rather I start on a bounded slice of this right now instead — say, just the honest renaming, or just the batch-execution redesign — tell me which one and I'll scope to that alone.

import time, json
from revision2.calibration_supervisor import CalibrationSupervisor, CalibrationRunConfig, AcceptanceGates
from market_data_loader import MarketDataLoader, SingleSymbolReplayFeed
from canonical_parameter_registry import CanonicalParameterRegistry

reg = CanonicalParameterRegistry()

cfg = CalibrationRunConfig(
    phase1_trials=12, phase2_generations=3, phase3_iterations=10, seed=101,

)

<cc-memory filenames="ecs-project-rigor-standard.md">Per your standing instruction not to weaken the gate just because candidates fail, I'm reporting this straight rather than dressing it up.</cc-memory>

```
68 candidates, all three phases, 52.3 minutes
stopped_reason: completed (not budget/error)
best_score: -inf → NO_ACCEPTABLE_CANDIDATE
```

| Phase | Candidates | Accepted | Best guidance score |
|---|---|---|---|
| random | 6 | 0 | -5.11 |
| TPE | 6 | 0 | -4.99 |
| CMA-ES | 45 | 0 | -4.75 |
| fine-tune | 11 | 0 | -4.66 |

### The fix is confirmed working
This is the outcome that matters most: guidance score improved **monotonically across every phase** (-5.11 → -4.99 → -4.75 → -4.66), and **all three search phases actually ran** — CMA-ES and fine-tuning never even started before your fix, because phase 1 had zero accepted candidates. Now they ran their full budget and made real, if modest, progress. Zero crashes across 68 real 48-symbol backtests. Checkpoint has all 68 candidates with full metrics.

### No bugs found
I watched every candidate live, and did one extra check when the profit-factor pattern looked suspiciously uniform (0.01–0.25 across dozens of independently-varied candidates): 79% of exits were stops (0% win rate by construction), only 6% hit target. That's not a code defect — it's the PA/ID/MPC heuristics genuinely not having demonstrated edge on this window with any parameter combination tried. Correctly and honestly reported as `NO_ACCEPTABLE_CANDIDATE` rather than papering over it.

### What this run does and doesn't tell you
- **Does**: prove the full pipeline — search, gates, checkpointing, portfolio backtesting — works correctly end-to-end under real adverse conditions.
- **Doesn't**: tell you whether the strategy has real edge. This was 600 bars (~2 weeks), 68 candidates, on one specific window — nowhere near enough to conclude "no edge exists." That question needs the longer window and larger trial budget you were already asking to benchmark toward.

Nothing to fix, nothing restarted. Ready for the next step whenever you want it.
